Learn English/Parts of speech/04-adjectives/catalog

More adjectives: Q–S#

Adjectives Q–S beyond the grammar groups — the everyday adjectives that a triage of WordNet’s adjective lemmas showed a learner needs.

← The grammar of adjectives · All groups

Most English adjectives behave: they go before a noun or after be, and they compare with -er or more. These are those — but each still says how it compares, where it can stand, and what it takes. WordNet lists about twenty thousand adjectives; most of the rest are technical, archaic or British-only, and a triage pass left them out on purpose.

Qatari#

Pronunciation
/kəˈtɑːri/ · kuh-TAH-ree
Русский
катарский
Type
origin
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Always capitalized. A Qatari for a person is a noun, not an adjective.

From Qatar, or relating to Qatar and its people.

  1. A Qatari airline sponsors the tournament.
  2. The stadium was built with Qatari money.
  3. My lab partner is Qatari.

quack#

Pronunciation
/kwæk/ · KWAK
Русский
шарлатанский, знахарский
Type
classifying; also evaluative
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Also a noun (a quack is a fake doctor) and a verb (ducks quack). As an adjective it goes only before a noun: say The remedy is a fraud, not The remedy is quack.

Falsely claiming to have medical knowledge or to cure people.

  1. He spent thousands on quack remedies.
  2. A quack doctor sold them fake vitamins.
  3. The clinic pushes quack cures for cancer.

quadratic#

Pronunciation
/kwɑːˈdrætɪk/ · kwah-DRAT-ik
Русский
квадратный (об уравнении), квадратичный
Type
classifying
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
not gradable

In math: involving a quantity multiplied by itself, that is, squared.

  1. We solved the quadratic equation in class.
  2. She still remembers the quadratic formula from tenth grade.
  3. The growth is quadratic, not linear.

quadrilateral#

Pronunciation
/ˌkwɑːdrɪˈlætərəl/ · kwah-dri-LAT-ur-uhl
Русский
четырёхсторонний, четырёхугольный
Type
shape
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
not gradable
Form
quadri- (four) + lateral (sided)
Contrast
Also a noun: a square is a quadrilateral.

Having four straight sides.

  1. A trapezoid is a quadrilateral shape.
  2. Draw any quadrilateral figure on the board.
  3. The old fort is roughly quadrilateral.

quadruple#

Pronunciation
/kwɑːˈdruːpəl/ · kwah-DROO-puhl
Русский
четырёхкратный, учетверённый
Type
quantity
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
quadru- (four) — compare double, triple
Contrast
Also a verb (Sales quadrupled last year). For the surgery and the crime the noun after it stays singular: a quadruple bypass, a quadruple homicide.

Made up of four parts, or four times as big or as many.

  1. He had a quadruple bypass last spring.
  2. Police are investigating a quadruple homicide.
  3. She landed a quadruple jump in competition.

quaint#

Pronunciation
/kweɪnt/ · KWAYNT
Русский
причудливый, старомодно-милый
Type
evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est or more/most — quainter, quaintest or more quaint, most quaint
Contrast
It can sound condescending: calling someone's beliefs quaint says they are charmingly out of date.

Quaint has 2 distinct senses.

1. Attractive in an old-fashioned way. (старомодно-милый, уютный)

  1. They stayed in a quaint little inn on the coast.
  2. It's the quaintest town in New England.

2. Odd or out of date in a way that amuses you. (причудливый, забавно устаревший)

  1. He has a quaint habit of writing thank-you letters.
  2. The idea of a landline seems quaint now.

qualitative#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɑːlɪˌteɪtɪv/ · KWAH-li-tay-tiv
Русский
качественный (не количественный)
Type
classifying
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
not gradable
Opposite
quantitative
Contrast
A false friend. Russian качественный usually means of good quality — that is quality or high-quality in English. Qualitative only contrasts with quantitative: about kind, not about amount.

Relating to the nature or kind of something rather than to amounts.

  1. The study used qualitative interviews instead of surveys.
  2. There is a qualitative difference between the two recordings.
  3. Her research is mostly qualitative.

quality#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɑːləti/ · KWAH-luh-tee
Русский
качественный, высокого качества
Type
evaluative
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Mostly a noun (the quality of the work). As an adjective it goes only before a noun; after a verb say high-quality: The tools are high-quality, not The tools are quality.

Of a high standard.

  1. They sell quality furniture at a fair price.
  2. We try to spend quality time together on weekends.
  3. Quality childcare is hard to find here.

quantifiable#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɑːntɪˌfaɪəbəl/ · KWAHN-ti-fy-uh-buhl
Русский
измеримый, поддающийся подсчёту
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more quantifiable, most quantifiable
Form
-able from quantify

Able to be measured or expressed in numbers.

  1. The benefits are real but not easily quantifiable.
  2. Give me quantifiable goals for the quarter.
  3. Air quality is more quantifiable than customer mood.

quantitative#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɑːntɪˌteɪtɪv/ · KWAHN-ti-tay-tiv
Русский
количественный
Type
classifying
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
not gradable
Opposite
qualitative

Relating to amounts and numbers.

  1. The report includes quantitative data from 3,000 users.
  2. He works on the quantitative side of the fund.
  3. Their evidence is quantitative rather than anecdotal.

quarantined#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɔːrənˌtiːnd/ · KWOR-uhn-teend
Русский
находящийся на карантине, изолированный
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ed from quarantine

Kept away from other people or animals so that a disease cannot spread.

  1. The quarantined passengers stayed on the ship for a week.
  2. Our whole class was quarantined after the outbreak.
  3. New birds are kept quarantined for thirty days.

quarrelsome#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɔːrəlsəm/ · KWOR-uhl-suhm
Русский
сварливый, склочный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more quarrelsome, most quarrelsome
Form
-some from quarrel

Arguing often, and looking for a fight.

  1. Two quarrelsome neighbors dragged the whole street into court.
  2. He gets quarrelsome after a couple of drinks.
  3. The committee is more quarrelsome than it used to be.

quarterly#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɔːrtərli/ · KWOR-tur-lee
Русский
ежеквартальный
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ly from quarter — an -ly adjective, not an adverb here
Contrast
Quarterly is also an adverb (The board meets quarterly) and a noun (a literary quarterly). As an adjective it stays before the noun: the quarterly report.

Happening or produced four times a year.

  1. The quarterly report comes out in April.
  2. Freelancers pay quarterly taxes.
  3. She writes for a quarterly literary magazine.

queasy#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwiːzi/ · KWEE-zee
Русский
испытывающий тошноту; неспокойный
Type
emotion; also quality
Position
both — mostly after a verb
Comparison
-er/-est — queasier, queasiest
Takes
about + noun or -ing

Queasy has 2 distinct senses.

1. Feeling as if you might vomit. (испытывающий тошноту)

  1. Boats make me queasy.
  2. I felt queasier with every curve in the road.

2. Uneasy and worried about something. (неспокойный, встревоженный)

  1. Investors are queasy about the merger.
  2. The whole plan gives me a queasy feeling.

queen-size#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwiːnˌsaɪz/ · KWEEN-syz
Русский
размера «квин» (кровать 152×203 см)
Type
size; also compound
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — queen + size; queen-sized is just as common
Contrast
A US bed-size word. From small to large: twin, full, queen-size, king-size. A queen-size bed is about 60 by 80 inches.

Of a bed or bedding: fairly large — bigger than a full, smaller than a king.

  1. The room has a queen-size bed.
  2. We need queen-size sheets, not king.
  3. A queen-size mattress won't fit up those stairs.

queer#

Pronunciation
/kwɪr/ · KWEER
Русский
квир, ЛГБТ; странный (устар.)
Type
classifying; also quality
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable — in the identity sense; the old strange sense took queerer, queerest
Contrast
Once a slur, now reclaimed and used neutrally by many LGBTQ people, in academic writing and in queer studies. Still, do not apply it to an individual who has not used it of themselves. The strange sense is old-fashioned and now sounds odd in American English.

Queer has 2 distinct senses.

1. Relating to people who are not heterosexual or not cisgender. (квир, ЛГБТ)

  1. She writes about queer history in the Midwest.
  2. The festival celebrates queer artists.
  3. He came out as queer in college.

2. Old-fashioned: strange or odd. (странный, чудной)

  1. There was something queer about the whole story.
  2. He felt a bit queer after the shot.

querulous#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɛrələs/ · KWER-uh-luhs
Русский
ворчливый, брюзгливый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more querulous, most querulous
Contrast
Not related to query. It describes a whiny, complaining tone, not a questioning one.

Complaining in a weak, irritated way.

  1. A querulous voice came from the back row.
  2. He grew querulous in his last years.
  3. Her emails get more querulous every week.

questionable#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɛstʃənəbəl/ · KWES-chuh-nuh-buhl
Русский
сомнительный, небезупречный
Type
evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more questionable, most questionable
Form
-able from question

Probably not true, honest or good — worth doubting.

  1. The numbers came from a questionable source.
  2. Several of the expenses look questionable.
  3. His taste in movies is even more questionable than mine.

questioning#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɛstʃənɪŋ/ · KWES-chuh-ning
Русский
вопросительный, недоумевающий
Type
participial
Position
attributive only
Comparison
more/most — more questioning, most questioning
Form
-ing from question

Showing that you have doubts or want an answer.

  1. She gave me a questioning look.
  2. He raised a questioning eyebrow.
  3. Students today take a more questioning attitude toward authority.

quick#

Pronunciation
/kwɪk/ · KWIK
Русский
быстрый; короткий; сообразительный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — quicker, quickest
Takes
to-infinitive — quick to blame
Opposite
slow
Contrast
The adverb is quickly. Come quick! is informal speech; in writing use quickly. Fast is about speed of movement, quick often about a short time: a fast car, a quick trip.

Quick has 3 distinct senses.

1. Moving or happening fast. (быстрый, скорый)

  1. She's a quick walker — I can never keep up.
  2. Is there a quicker way downtown?
  3. He's always quick to blame someone else.

2. Taking only a short time. (короткий, быстрый (недолгий))

  1. I took a quick shower and left.
  2. Can we have a quick word before the meeting?

3. Able to understand and learn fast. (сообразительный)

  1. She's quick — she picked up the software in a day.
  2. He has a quick mind and a sharp tongue.

quick-tempered#

Pronunciation
/ˌkwɪkˈtɛmpərd/ · kwik-TEM-purd
Русский
вспыльчивый
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more quick-tempered, most quick-tempered
Form
compound — adjective + noun + -ed
Opposite
even-tempered

Getting angry very easily.

  1. His father was quick-tempered and everyone in town knew it.
  2. She's less quick-tempered than she was at twenty.
  3. A quick-tempered coach can lose a locker room fast.

quick-witted#

Pronunciation
/ˌkwɪkˈwɪtɪd/ · kwik-WIT-id
Русский
находчивый, остроумный
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more quick-witted, most quick-witted
Form
compound — adjective + noun + -ed
Opposite
slow-witted

Able to think and answer fast, often in a funny way.

  1. The host is quick-witted and never loses the room.
  2. A quick-witted clerk stopped the scam.
  3. You have to be more quick-witted than that to do stand-up.

quilted#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɪltɪd/ · KWIL-tid
Русский
стёганый
Type
material; also participial
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ed from quilt

Made of two layers of cloth sewn together with padding in between.

  1. She wore a quilted jacket over her sweater.
  2. The bedspread is quilted by hand.
  3. Quilted oven mitts protect your hands better.

quintessential#

Pronunciation
/ˌkwɪntɪˈsɛnʃəl/ · kwin-ti-SEN-shuhl
Русский
типичнейший, воплощающий суть
Type
evaluative
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable — it already means the most typical
Form
from quintessence — the purest form of something

Being the most perfect example of a type.

  1. He's the quintessential New Yorker — loud, kind, always late.
  2. Diners are the quintessential American restaurant.
  3. That album is quintessential seventies rock.

quirky#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɜːrki/ · KWUR-kee
Русский
чудаковатый, с причудами
Type
evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — quirkier, quirkiest
Form
-y from quirk

Unusual in a likable, slightly odd way.

  1. It's a quirky little bookstore with a cat in the window.
  2. Her second novel is even quirkier than the first.
  3. He has a quirky sense of humor.

quixotic#

Pronunciation
/kwɪkˈsɑːtɪk/ · kwik-SAH-tik
Русский
донкихотский, прекраснодушный
Type
evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more quixotic, most quixotic
Form
from Don Quixote
Contrast
The pronunciation does not follow the name: kwik-SAH-tik, not kee-HOH-tik.

Having generous, idealistic plans that have almost no chance of working.

  1. He launched a quixotic run for governor.
  2. The plan is noble but more quixotic than practical.
  3. Her quixotic campaign to save the old theater lasted two years.

quizzical#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɪzɪkəl/ · KWIZ-i-kuhl
Русский
недоумевающий, вопросительный
Type
quality
Position
attributive only
Comparison
more/most — more quizzical, most quizzical

Showing that you are puzzled, often with some amusement.

  1. He gave her a quizzical look.
  2. She raised one eyebrow in a quizzical expression.
  3. The second question got an even more quizzical look than the first.

quotable#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwoʊtəbəl/ · KWOH-tuh-buhl
Русский
меткий, годный для цитирования
Type
evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more quotable, most quotable
Form
-able from quote

Worth quoting, or saying things that people like to repeat.

  1. The coach is endlessly quotable.
  2. It's the most quotable movie of the decade.
  3. He wrote a short, quotable line for the poster.

rabble-rousing#

Pronunciation
/ˈræbəlˌraʊzɪŋ/ · RAB-uhl-row-zing
Русский
подстрекательский, демагогический
Type
compound; also participial
Position
attributive only
Comparison
more/most — more rabble-rousing, most rabble-rousing
Form
compound — noun + -ing participle; a rabble is a disorderly crowd

Stirring up the anger of a crowd, usually for political ends.

  1. He gave a rabble-rousing speech outside the capitol.
  2. The paper called her a rabble-rousing populist.
  3. His second speech was even more rabble-rousing than the first.

rabid#

Pronunciation
/ˈræbɪd/ · RAB-id
Русский
ярый, фанатичный; бешеный (о животном)
Type
quality
Position
both — the rabies sense mostly before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more rabid, most rabid (the fanatical sense only)

Rabid has 2 distinct senses.

1. Extreme and fanatical in your opinions or support. (ярый, фанатичный)

  1. He's a rabid Yankees fan.
  2. The bill faces rabid opposition in the Senate.
  3. Her most rabid supporters lined up at five in the morning.

2. Of an animal: having rabies. (бешеный (заражённый бешенством))

  1. A rabid raccoon bit the neighbor's dog.
  2. Animal control caught a rabid fox behind the school.

racial#

Pronunciation
/ˈreɪʃəl/ · RAY-shuhl
Русский
расовый
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-al from race
Contrast
Racial is neutral and simply names the category (racial demographics). Racist is a judgment: it means based on prejudice. A racial comment is not the same as a racist comment.

Relating to race, or to relations between races.

  1. The city studied racial disparities in housing.
  2. Racial profiling is against department policy.
  3. They talked about racial justice in the classroom.

racist#

Pronunciation
/ˈreɪsɪst/ · RAY-sist
Русский
расистский
Type
evaluative; also classifying
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more racist, most racist
Form
-ist from race
Contrast
Also a noun (He's a racist). Keep it apart from the neutral racial.

Showing or based on prejudice against people of another race.

  1. He was fired for a racist joke.
  2. The policy had a racist effect, whatever its intent.
  3. His later columns were even more openly racist.

racy#

Pronunciation
/ˈreɪsi/ · RAY-see
Русский
пикантный, фривольный
Type
evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — racier, raciest
Contrast
Nothing to do with race the contest or race the group of people — it means mildly sexual.

Slightly shocking, usually because of sexual content.

  1. The novel was considered racy in 1955.
  2. She posted a racier photo than usual.
  3. It's a racy comedy — don't watch it with your parents.

radiant#

Pronunciation
/ˈreɪdiənt/ · RAY-dee-uhnt
Русский
сияющий, лучезарный; лучистый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more radiant, most radiant
Takes
with + noun — radiant with joy

Radiant has 2 distinct senses.

1. Looking very happy, in a way that shows on your face. (сияющий, лучезарный)

  1. The bride looked radiant.
  2. She gave us the most radiant smile.
  3. He was radiant with pride at the ceremony.

2. Sending out light or heat. (лучистый, излучающий)

  1. Radiant heat from the floor warms the whole room.
  2. The radiant glow of the fire filled the cabin.

radical#

Pronunciation
/ˈrædɪkəl/ · RAD-i-kuhl
Русский
радикальный, коренной; крайний (полит.)
Type
quality; also classifying
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more radical, most radical
Contrast
Also a noun (a radical). Beyond the political meaning that Russian радикальный shares, English radical often just means thorough and far-reaching: a radical redesign is not a political statement.

Radical has 2 distinct senses.

1. Complete and far-reaching, especially of a change. (коренной, кардинальный)

  1. The company made radical changes to the schedule.
  2. We need a more radical rethink of the budget.
  3. She had radical surgery and recovered well.

2. Supporting extreme political or social change. (радикальный, крайний)

  1. She joined a radical student group in the seventies.
  2. His views got more radical over the years.

radio-controlled#

Pronunciation
/ˈreɪdioʊkənˌtroʊld/ · RAY-dee-oh-kuhn-trohld
Русский
радиоуправляемый
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — noun + participle; often shortened to RC

Operated from a distance by radio signals.

  1. He flies a radio-controlled plane on weekends.
  2. The gate at the end of the driveway is radio-controlled.
  3. Radio-controlled cars were the big gift that Christmas.

radioactive#

Pronunciation
/ˌreɪdioʊˈæktɪv/ · ray-dee-oh-AK-tiv
Русский
радиоактивный; политически опасный
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more radioactive, most radioactive

Radioactive has 2 distinct senses.

1. Giving off harmful radiation. (радиоактивный)

  1. The site is still radioactive forty years later.
  2. They store the radioactive waste underground.
  3. Some of the soil is more radioactive than the air above it.

2. So controversial that politicians avoid touching it. (политически опасный, «токсичный»)

  1. Social Security is radioactive in an election year.
  2. The topic became radioactive after the scandal.

ragged#

Pronunciation
/ˈræɡɪd/ · RAG-id
Русский
рваный, потрёпанный; неровный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more ragged, most ragged
Contrast
Two syllables: RAG-id, like rugged and wicked. In run ragged it means worn out from overwork: The twins run me ragged.

Ragged has 2 distinct senses.

1. Torn, old and in bad condition. (рваный, потрёпанный)

  1. He wore a ragged T-shirt and no shoes.
  2. The flag was ragged at the edges after the storm.

2. Uneven and rough, not smooth or controlled. (неровный, сбивчивый)

  1. The team's play was ragged in the first quarter.
  2. She took a ragged breath and kept going.
  3. His singing got more ragged toward the end of the tour.

raging#

Pronunciation
/ˈreɪdʒɪŋ/ · RAY-jing
Русский
бушующий, яростный; сильный (о боли)
Type
participial
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ing from rage

Raging has 2 distinct senses.

1. Very strong and violent, of fire, water or weather. (бушующий, свирепствующий)

  1. A raging fire destroyed the barn.
  2. They had to cross a raging river.

2. Very intense, of pain, thirst or an argument. (сильный, ожесточённый)

  1. I woke up with a raging headache.
  2. There's a raging debate about the new rules.

rainy#

Pronunciation
/ˈreɪni/ · RAY-nee
Русский
дождливый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — rainier, rainiest
Form
-y from rain
Contrast
Save it for a rainy day means save money for a time when you will need it.

With a lot of rain.

  1. It was a cold, rainy Tuesday in March.
  2. October was rainier than usual this year.
  3. The whole week is supposed to be rainy.

raised#

Pronunciation
/reɪzd/ · RAYZD
Русский
приподнятый, возвышающийся
Type
participial
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ed from raise
Contrast
Raised voices means loud and angry ones. She was raised in Ohio is the verb, not this adjective.

Higher than the surface around it.

  1. We built a raised garden bed out of cedar.
  2. The band played on a raised platform at the end of the room.
  3. Raised lettering runs across the cover.

rambling#

Pronunciation
/ˈræmblɪŋ/ · RAM-bling
Русский
бессвязный, многословный; беспорядочно раскинувшийся
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more rambling, most rambling
Form
-ing from ramble

Rambling has 2 distinct senses.

1. Long and confused, with no clear point. (бессвязный, многословный)

  1. He left a rambling voicemail at two in the morning.
  2. The essay is rambling and hard to follow.
  3. Her second speech was even more rambling than the first.

2. Of a house or garden: spread out in an irregular way. (беспорядочно раскинувшийся)

  1. They bought a rambling old farmhouse with six bedrooms.
  2. A rambling rose covers the back fence.

random#

Pronunciation
/ˈrændəm/ · RAN-duhm

Contrast
At random is a fixed phrase: We picked names at random. The informal strange sense is common in speech but out of place in formal writing.

Random has 2 distinct senses.

1. Chosen or happening with no plan or pattern. (случайный, произвольный)

  1. They called ten random names from the list.

2. Informal: strange, unexpected or out of place. (странный, ни с того ни с сего)

  1. Some random guy started talking to me on the bus.
  2. That was the most random thing he's ever said.

Pronunciation

Rank has 2 distinct senses.

2. Smelling strong and unpleasant. (вонючий, затхлый)

  1. His gym bag smelled even more rank after a week in the car.
  2. Rank water sat in the bottom of the cooler.

Russian редкий also covers hair and combs; English uses thin for hair (thinning hair) and fine-toothed for combs. For meat the scale is rare, medium rare, medium, medium well, well done.

Rare has 2 distinct senses.

2. Of beef: cooked only a little, still red inside. (с кровью, полусырой)

  1. He orders his steak rare.

raw#

Pronunciation
/rɔː/ · RAW
Русский
сырой; необработанный; содранный; неприкрытый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — rawer, rawest
Contrast
A raw deal means unfair treatment. Raw meat is uncooked; meat that has gone bad is spoiled.

Raw has 4 distinct senses.

1. Not cooked. (сырой, невареный)

  1. Don't eat raw cookie dough.
  2. The fish is served raw, with rice and ginger.

2. Not yet processed, cleaned up or analyzed. (необработанный, исходный)

  1. The lab sent us the raw data.
  2. Raw materials cost more this year than last.

3. Of skin: sore, red and painful. (содранный, саднящий)

  1. His hands were raw from the rope.
  2. My throat felt raw after shouting all night.

4. Honest and full of strong feeling, with nothing softened. (неприкрытый, откровенный)

  1. It's the rawest song on the album.
  2. The interview was raw and uncomfortable to watch.

Reactive has 2 distinct senses.

1. Responding to events after they happen instead of planning ahead. (реагирующий постфактум, несистемный)

  1. Management is reactive — nothing gets fixed until it breaks.

2. In chemistry: easily taking part in chemical reactions. (химически активный)

  1. Sodium is highly reactive with water.
  2. Store the reactive chemicals in a separate cabinet.

real#

Pronunciation
/ˈriːəl/ · REE-uhl
Русский
настоящий, реальный; подлинный; сущий
Type
quality; also emphasizing
Position
both — the emphasizing sense only before a noun: a real problem
Comparison
more/most — more real, most real
Opposite
fake
Contrast
Real good and real fast are informal speech; in writing use the adverb really. For real is also informal.

Real has 3 distinct senses.

1. Actually existing, not imagined or invented. (реальный, существующий)

  1. Is the threat real, or is it just talk?
  2. The dream felt more real than the morning did.

2. Genuine, not a copy or an imitation. (настоящий, подлинный)

  1. The bag is real leather.
  2. Those aren't real diamonds.

3. Used before a noun to stress how much something is what it is. (сущий, настоящий (усилительное))

  1. The parking downtown is a real problem.
  2. He can be a real pain when he's tired.

realized#

Pronunciation
/ˈriːəlaɪzd/ · REE-uh-lyzd
Русский
воплощённый; реализованный (о прибыли)
Type
participial
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ed from realize; American spelling -ized
Contrast
Realize is usually a verb (I realized my mistake). As an adjective it turns up in fully realized and in finance, where realized gains are ones you have actually taken.

Realized has 2 distinct senses.

1. Made real and worked out in full. (воплощённый, законченный)

  1. It's a fully realized vision of the city in 2100.
  2. The characters feel like realized people, not sketches.

2. In finance: actually received or lost, not just on paper. (реализованный (о прибыли, убытке))

  1. You pay tax on realized gains, not on paper ones.
  2. Realized losses can offset those gains.

rear#

Pronunciation
/rɪr/ · REER
Русский
задний
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Opposite
front
Contrast
Also a noun (the rear of the building) and a verb (to rear children). As an adjective it never follows a verb: say The door at the back is locked, not The door is rear.

At or in the back of something.

  1. The rear door doesn't lock properly.
  2. Passengers exit through the rear entrance.
  3. Check the rear tires for wear.

reasonable#

Pronunciation
/ˈriːzənəbəl/ · REE-zuh-nuh-buhl
Русский
разумный; приемлемый; недорогой
Type
evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more reasonable, most reasonable
Form
-able from reason
Opposite
unreasonable

Reasonable has 3 distinct senses.

1. Fair and sensible. (разумный, справедливый)

  1. That sounds like a reasonable request.
  2. Be reasonable — he's only nine.
  3. A more reasonable boss would have let it go.

2. Fairly good or fairly large, though not outstanding. (неплохой, изрядный)

  1. We had a reasonable turnout for a rainy night.
  2. She has a reasonable chance of winning the seat.

3. Not too expensive. (недорогой, умеренный (о цене))

  1. The hotel was clean and reasonable.
  2. They charge reasonable prices for repairs.

reasoned#

Pronunciation
/ˈriːzənd/ · REE-zuhnd
Русский
обоснованный, аргументированный
Type
participial
Position
attributive only
Comparison
more/most — more reasoned, most reasoned
Form
-ed from reason
Contrast
Reasoned is about the argument — carefully built. Reasonable is about the person or the demand — fair and sensible.

Based on careful thinking and clear argument.

  1. She wrote a reasoned response to the criticism.
  2. The judge gave a carefully reasoned opinion.
  3. We need a more reasoned debate about the bill.

reassured#

Pronunciation
/ˌriːəˈʃʊrd/ · ree-uh-SHUURD
Русский
успокоенный, ободрённый
Type
emotion; also participial
Position
both — mostly after a verb
Comparison
more/most — more reassured, most reassured
Takes
about or by + noun; that-clause
Form
-ed — how the person feels
Contrast
reassured is how you feel; reassuring is the thing that causes it: I felt reassured because her voice was reassuring.

No longer worried, because someone has taken your fear away.

  1. She felt reassured after talking to the doctor.
  2. Everyone left the meeting reassured about their jobs.
  3. I was more reassured by her calm tone than by anything she actually said.

reassuring#

Pronunciation
/ˌriːəˈʃʊrɪŋ/ · ree-uh-SHUUR-ing
Русский
успокаивающий, обнадёживающий
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more reassuring, most reassuring
Form
-ing — what causes the feeling
Opposite
alarming
Contrast
The -ing form describes the thing that calms you; the -ed form describes the person: a reassuring nurse, a reassured patient.

Making someone feel less worried or afraid.

  1. Her voice on the phone was calm and reassuring.
  2. He gave the crowd a reassuring smile.
  3. The nurse's explanation was far more reassuring than the leaflet.

received#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈsiːvd/ · rih-SEEVD
Русский
общепринятый, устоявшийся
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
As an adjective it lives in a few fixed phrases — received wisdom, received opinion, received ideas. In I received your email it is just the verb.

Generally accepted by most people, though not necessarily true.

  1. The received wisdom is that you should never put everything in one stock.
  2. She spent her career challenging received ideas about diet.
  3. That view has hardened into received opinion in the field.

reconciled#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛkənsaɪld/ · REK-uhn-syld
Русский
смирившийся; помирившийся
Type
participial
Position
predicative only — after be, become or seem
Comparison
more/most — more reconciled, most reconciled
Takes
to + noun or -ing (accepting); with + person (friendly again)
Form
-ed from reconcile

Reconciled has 2 distinct senses.

1. Having accepted something unwelcome that cannot be changed. (смирившийся (с чем-то))

  1. She is reconciled to living alone.
  2. He seems reconciled to the fact that he will never play again.
  3. I am more reconciled to the move than I was in January.

2. Friendly again after a quarrel. (помирившийся)

  1. The brothers were finally reconciled after ten years of silence.
  2. She hoped to be reconciled with her father before he died.

recorded#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈkɔːrdɪd/ · rih-KOR-did
Русский
записанный, в записи; зафиксированный
Type
participial
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ed from record
Opposite
live

Recorded has 2 distinct senses.

1. Captured on tape, video or a computer so it can be played later. (записанный, в записи)

  1. They played recorded music instead of hiring a band.
  2. This call may be recorded for training purposes.
  3. He left a recorded message for the whole staff.

2. Written down in an official record. (зафиксированный, отмеченный в документах)

  1. It was the warmest June in recorded history.
  2. There is no recorded case of the disease in this state.

Able to be got back after being lost, spent or deleted.

recovered#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈkʌvərd/ · rih-KUHV-urd
Русский
выздоровевший; найденный, возвращённый
Type
participial
Position
both — mostly after a verb
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ed from recover

Recovered has 2 distinct senses.

1. Healthy again after an illness or injury. (выздоровевший, поправившийся)

  1. She is fully recovered from the surgery.
  2. He looks recovered, but he still tires by noon.

2. Found and brought back after being lost or stolen. (найденный, возвращённый)

  1. Police returned the recovered jewelry to its owner.
  2. The recovered files filled three hard drives.

recovering#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈkʌvərɪŋ/ · rih-KUHV-ur-ing
Русский
выздоравливающий; завязавший (об алкоголике)
Type
participial
Position
attributive only — after a verb it is the verb recover, not an adjective: She is recovering well
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ing from recover
Contrast
A recovering alcoholic keeps the -ing form for life — recovered would suggest the problem is over and gone.

Getting better after an illness, an injury or an addiction.

  1. He is a recovering alcoholic and has been sober for six years.
  2. The ward has a quiet lounge for recovering patients.
  3. She counsels recovering addicts at a community center.

red-faced#

Pronunciation
/ˌrɛdˈfeɪst/ · red-FAYST
Русский

Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable — usually intensified with very or increasingly
Form
compound — adjective + noun + -ed

Red-faced has 2 distinct senses.

1. Embarrassed, especially in public. (сконфуженный, смущённый)

  1. The mayor was left red-faced when the numbers turned out to be wrong.
  2. A red-faced official admitted the mistake to reporters.

2. With a red face from anger, heat or hard effort. (покрасневший, багровый)

  1. He came in red-faced and out of breath.
  2. The coach was red-faced with rage by halftime.

Red-hot has 2 distinct senses.

2. Very exciting, very successful or very much in demand right now. (очень популярный, горячий, злободневный)

  1. She is the red-hot favorite for the award.
  2. The company's stock was red-hot all last quarter.

Good enough to make up for everything that is bad about something.

  3. The apartment is tiny, but the view is its redeeming feature.

redundant#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈdʌndənt/ · rih-DUHN-duhnt
Русский

Type

Position
both
Comparison

Contrast
In British English made redundant means laid off from a job. Americans do not use it that way — they say laid off.

Redundant has 2 distinct senses.

1. Repeating something that has already been said, and so not needed. (излишний, избыточный)

  1. Saying free gift is redundant — a gift is already free.
  2. Cut the second paragraph; it is redundant.
  3. The report gets more redundant with every draft.

2. Built in twice on purpose, as a backup. (дублирующий, резервный)

  1. The plane has redundant control systems.
  2. Customer data is stored on redundant servers in two states.

regular#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛɡjələr/ · REG-yuh-lur
Русский
регулярный; обычный; постоянный
Type
quality; also classifying
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more regular, most regular
Opposite
irregular

Regular has 4 distinct senses.

1. Happening again and again at even intervals. (регулярный, равномерный)

  1. Take the medicine at regular intervals, about six hours apart.
  2. Her heartbeat is strong and regular now.
  3. His visits have become more regular since he retired.

2. Normal or standard, not special and not large. (обычный, обыкновенный)

  1. I will just have a regular coffee, nothing fancy.
  2. It is not a holiday — it is a regular workday.

3. Coming to the same place or doing the same thing often. (постоянный (о клиенте, посетителе))

  1. He is one of our regular customers.
  2. The bar has a few regular patrons who come every night.

4. Grammar: following the normal pattern. (правильный (о глаголе, форме))

  1. Walk is a regular verb — its past is walked.
  2. Most English plurals are regular and simply add an s.

Relative has 2 distinct senses.

Formal: careless about a duty you should have carried out.

  1. It would be remiss of me not to thank the volunteers.
  2. The inspector was remiss in not checking the wiring.
  3. I would be remiss if I did not mention her help.

remorseless#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈmɔːrsləs/ · rih-MORS-luhs

Remorseless has 2 distinct senses.

1. Feeling no guilt at all about the harm you cause. (безжалостный, без угрызений совести)

  1. The killer was cold and remorseless on the stand.
  2. He seemed more remorseless with every question the prosecutor asked.

2. Never letting up. (неумолимый, непрекращающийся)

  1. The remorseless rain went on for five days.
  2. There is a remorseless logic to her argument.

remote#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈmoʊt/ · rih-MOHT
Русский
отдалённый, удалённый; ничтожный (о шансе); отчуждённый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est or more/most — remoter or more remote, remotest or most remote
Takes
from + noun

Remote has 4 distinct senses.

1. Far away from towns and from other people. (отдалённый, глухой)

  1. They live on a remote island off the coast of Maine.
  2. The cabin was more remote than the photos suggested.

2. Done from home or from another city instead of at the office. (удалённый, дистанционный)

  1. The job is fully remote, with two trips a year.
  2. She has had a remote position since 2020.

3. Of a chance or possibility: very small. (ничтожный, крайне малый)

  1. There is not the remotest chance he will agree.
  2. The risk is remote but it is real.

4. Distant and hard to get close to, in manner. (отчуждённый, холодный)

  1. He can seem remote until you know him well.
  2. Her father was a remote figure throughout her childhood.

устойчивый, стойкий; сопротивляющийся
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more resistant, most resistant
Takes
to + noun
Form
also the second half of compounds: heat-resistant, water-resistant

Resistant has 2 distinct senses.

1. Not harmed or affected by something. (устойчивый, стойкий)

  1. These bacteria are resistant to most antibiotics.
  2. Choose a stain-resistant fabric for the couch.

2. Unwilling to accept something and quietly fighting it. (сопротивляющийся, не принимающий)

  1. Older staff were resistant to the change.
  2. He is resistant to any kind of therapy.

Position

Firm about what you have decided, and not changing your mind.

  1. She was resolute in her decision to leave.
  2. The mayor stayed resolute under a week of pressure.
  3. He gave a resolute answer: no.

resonant#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛzənənt/ · REZ-uh-nuhnt
Русский

Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more resonant, most resonant
Takes

Resonant has 2 distinct senses.

1. Deep, clear and continuing, of a sound or a voice. (звучный, глубокий)

  1. He has a deep, resonant voice.
  2. The old bell gave a resonant hum.

2. Full of a meaning that stays with people. (созвучный, находящий отклик)

  1. The film is resonant with memories of childhood.
  2. Her speech was especially resonant for young voters.

resounding#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈzaʊndɪŋ/ · ri-ZOWN-ding
Русский
полный, безоговорочный; оглушительный
Type

Position

Comparison

Resounding has 2 distinct senses.

1. Complete and unmistakable, of a success, a defeat or an answer. (полный, безоговорочный)

  1. The show was a resounding success.
  2. The proposal met with a resounding no.

2. Loud and echoing. (оглушительный, раскатистый)

  1. There was a resounding crash from the kitchen.
  2. The speech got a resounding cheer.

Belonging to each of the people or things just named, separately.

Responsive has 2 distinct senses.

restive#

Close to restless, but restive adds impatience with being held back or controlled: a restive crowd is about to stop obeying.

Unwilling to keep waiting or to be controlled, and beginning to show it.

restless#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛstləs/ · REST-luhs

Type

Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more restless, most restless
Form

Restless has 2 distinct senses.

1. Unable to stay still or be patient, because you are bored or worried. (беспокойный, неугомонный)

  1. The kids get restless on long drives.
  2. He was restless all evening before the interview.

2. Broken and without real rest, of a night or of sleep. (беспокойный (о сне, о ночи))

  1. She had a restless night.
  2. It was a restless sleep full of strange dreams.

Restorative has 2 distinct senses.

  1. A hot bath is wonderfully restorative.
  2. She took a restorative week off in the mountains.

2. Aimed at repairing harm rather than punishing. (восстановительный (о правосудии, лечении))

  1. The school uses restorative justice instead of suspensions.
  2. He does restorative dental work.

  1. The retiring chairman gave a short speech.

rhetorical#

Pronunciation

Position

Comparison

Rhetorical has 2 distinct senses.

1. Asked for effect, not because you want an answer — of a question. (риторический (о вопросе))

  1. Don't answer — it was a rhetorical question.
  2. He ended with a rhetorical question about fairness.

2. Having to do with the art of speaking and writing persuasively. (риторический, ораторский)

  1. It is a rhetorical device, not a real promise.
  2. Her rhetorical skill won the debate.

riddled#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɪdəld/ · RID-uhld
Русский

Riddled has 2 distinct senses.

  2. The whole system is riddled with corruption.

rough#

Pronunciation
/rʌf/ · RUHF
Русский
шероховатый; тяжёлый; приблизительный; грубый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — rougher, roughest
Opposite
smooth
Contrast
The gh is pronounced /f/: rough rhymes with stuff. The adverb roughly also means 'approximately': roughly ten miles.

Rough has 4 distinct senses.

1. Not smooth or even to the touch. (шероховатый, неровный)

  1. The wall was rough to the touch.
  2. My hands get rough in the winter.
  3. This road is rougher than the one we took.

2. Difficult or unpleasant to live through. (тяжёлый, трудный)

  1. She's had a rough year.
  2. It was a rough flight.

3. Approximate; not finished or exact. (приблизительный, черновой)

  1. Give me a rough estimate.
  2. This is just a rough draft.

4. Using force; not gentle or safe. (грубый, буйный)

  1. Don't be too rough with the puppy.
  2. The neighborhood used to be pretty rough.

rough-and-tumble#

Pronunciation
/ˌrʌf ən ˈtʌmbəl/ · ruhf-uhn-TUHM-buhl
Русский
грубоватый, беспорядочный; жёсткий
Type
compound
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — adjective + and + verb

Rough and disorderly, but usually without real harm.

  1. Rough-and-tumble play is normal for puppies.
  2. He grew up in a rough-and-tumble neighborhood.
  3. She loves the rough-and-tumble world of city politics.

round-the-clock#

Pronunciation
/ˌraʊnd ðə ˈklɑːk/ · rownd-thuh-KLAHK
Русский
круглосуточный
Type
compound
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — preposition phrase used as one word
Contrast
Before a noun it is the adjective round-the-clock care; after a verb use the adverb around the clock: They worked around the clock.

Going on all day and all night without stopping.

  1. The patient needs round-the-clock care.
  2. There was round-the-clock coverage of the trial.
  3. The plant runs on a round-the-clock schedule.

roundabout#

Pronunciation
/ˈraʊndəbaʊt/ · ROWN-duh-bowt
Русский
окольный, обходной; уклончивый
Type
quality
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
In Britain a roundabout is a noun — a traffic circle. As an adjective meaning 'indirect' it is normal American English.

Not direct — going the long way, or said in an indirect way.

  1. We took a roundabout route to avoid the highway.
  2. He told me in a roundabout way that I hadn't gotten the job.
  3. That's a roundabout explanation for something very simple.

rounded#

Pronunciation
/ˈraʊndɪd/ · ROWN-did
Русский
закруглённый; всесторонний
Type
shape; also participial
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more rounded, most rounded
Form
-ed from round

Rounded has 2 distinct senses.

1. Curved, with no sharp corners or edges. (закруглённый)

  1. The table has rounded corners.
  2. Her handwriting is soft and rounded.
  3. Sand the edges until they're rounded.

2. Complete and balanced, covering every side of something. (всесторонний, разносторонний)

  1. The school aims to give kids a rounded education.
  2. She wanted a more rounded view of the problem.

rousing#

Pronunciation
/ˈraʊzɪŋ/ · ROW-zing
Русский

Type
participial; also evaluative
Position
attributive only
Comparison
more/most — more rousing, most rousing
Form
-ing from rouse

Making people excited and enthusiastic.

  1. The coach gave a rousing speech at halftime.
  2. The band ended with a rousing version of the anthem.
  3. The bill passed to rousing applause.

routine#

Pronunciation
/ruːˈtiːn/ · roo-TEEN
Русский
обычный, плановый, рутинный
Type
classifying
Position
both — mostly before a noun: a routine checkup
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
The stress is on the second syllable: roo-TEEN. A routine is also a noun — a fixed order of doing things, or a dance or comedy act.

Done regularly as normal procedure; ordinary.

  1. It was a routine traffic stop.
  2. She's in for routine surgery on Thursday.
  3. There is nothing routine about this case.

roving#

Pronunciation
/ˈroʊvɪŋ/ · ROH-ving
Русский
странствующий, разъездной
Type
participial
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ing from rove
Contrast
A roving eye is a set phrase for someone who keeps looking at other people romantically.

Moving from place to place instead of staying in one.

  1. She works as a roving reporter for the network.
  2. A roving band of musicians played between the tables.
  3. The museum sends a roving exhibit to small towns.

rowdy#

Русский

quality

Noisy and rough, in a way that can cause trouble.

  1. A rowdy group of fans filled the bar.

  3. It was a rowdy wedding, in the best way.

royal#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɔɪəl/ · ROY-uhl
Русский
королевский; (разг.) полный, страшный
Type
classifying; also emphasizing
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Royal means 'of a king or queen'; regal means 'looking like royalty'. A royal pain has nothing to do with kings — it just means a very big nuisance.

Royal has 2 distinct senses.

1. Belonging to or connected with a king or queen. (королевский)

  1. The royal family spent the summer in Scotland.
  2. The palace is still a royal residence.
  3. She received a royal pardon.

2. Complete, total — used before a few words to make them stronger. (полный, страшный)

  1. Parking downtown is a royal pain.
  2. He made a royal mess of the kitchen.

rubbery#

Pronunciation

Type
quality
Position

Comparison
more/most — more rubbery, most rubbery
Form
-y from rubber
Contrast
A rubber mat is made of rubber; rubbery chicken only feels like it.

Tough and bendy like rubber, usually said about badly cooked food.

  1. The calamari was rubbery.
  2. Microwaved eggs come out rubbery.
  3. He cut into a rubbery piece of steak.

Red and healthy-looking, said about skin.

rudimentary#

Pronunciation

Русский

Very basic — only the first and simplest level.

  1. He has a rudimentary knowledge of Spanish.
  2. The clinic has only rudimentary equipment.
  3. Our plan is still pretty rudimentary.

ruffled#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʌfəld/ · RUH-fuhld
Русский
взъерошенный; встревоженный; с оборками
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more ruffled, most ruffled
Form
-ed from ruffle
Contrast
Unruffled is the common opposite of the second sense: She stayed unruffled through the whole meeting.

Ruffled has 3 distinct senses.

1. Pushed out of place so it is no longer smooth. (взъерошенный, растрёпанный)

  1. The bird sat there with ruffled feathers.
  2. His hair was ruffled from the wind.

2. Slightly upset or annoyed. (встревоженный, задетый)

  1. She looked a little ruffled by the question.
  2. Nothing gets him ruffled.

3. Made with ruffles — strips of gathered cloth. (с оборками)

  1. She wore a white ruffled blouse.
  2. The dress has a ruffled hem.

rugged#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʌɡɪd/ · RUH-gid
Русский
суровый, неровный; крепкий
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more rugged, most rugged
Contrast
The -ed is always /ɪd/ and makes a second syllable: RUH-gid, never ruhgd.

Rugged has 3 distinct senses.

1. Rough, rocky and hard to travel across. (неровный, труднопроходимый)

  1. The rugged coastline is hard to reach by car.
  2. We hiked through rugged terrain for six hours.

2. Strong and built to survive hard use. (крепкий, прочный)

  1. These boots are rugged enough for winter work.
  2. The laptop comes in a rugged case.

3. Of a man's face: strong-featured in an attractive way. (мужественный (о лице))

  1. He has a rugged, weather-beaten face.
  2. The ad was looking for a rugged type.

ruined#

Pronunciation
/ˈruːɪnd/ · ROO-ind
Русский
разрушенный; испорченный
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ed from ruin

Ruined has 2 distinct senses.

1. Destroyed, with only broken remains left. (разрушенный)

  1. We walked around a ruined castle above the river.
  2. The ruined church is still standing after the fire.

2. Spoiled so badly that it is no good any more. (испорченный)

  1. My shoes were ruined by the road salt.
  2. The whole evening was ruined by one phone call.

Causing very serious damage, especially to money or a business.

  1. The lawsuit was ruinous for the family business.
  2. They borrowed at ruinous interest rates.
  3. A second bad harvest would be ruinous.

A ruling is also a noun — a judge's or an official's decision.

Having power over a country or an organization.

  1. The ruling party lost its majority.
  2. Members of the ruling class sent their children abroad.
  3. The ruling coalition fell apart in March.

run-down#

Pronunciation
/ˌrʌn ˈdaʊn/ · ruhn-DOWN
Русский
запущенный, обветшалый; измотанный
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more run-down, most run-down
Form
compound — verb + particle
Contrast
A rundown, written as one word with the stress on run, is a noun meaning a quick summary: Give me the rundown.

Run-down has 2 distinct senses.

1. In bad condition because nobody has taken care of it. (запущенный, обветшалый)

  1. They bought a run-down house on the edge of town.
  2. The whole block looks run-down.

2. Tired and slightly unwell. (измотанный, ослабленный)

  1. I've been feeling run-down since the trip.
  2. You look run-down — take a day off.

run-of-the-mill#

Pronunciation
/ˌrʌn əv ðə ˈmɪl/ · ruhn-uhv-thuh-MIL
Русский
заурядный, обычный
Type
compound; also evaluative
Position

Opposite

Ordinary, with nothing special about it.

  1. It's a run-of-the-mill office job.
  2. The food was run-of-the-mill diner food.
  3. This is no run-of-the-mill thunderstorm.

run-on#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʌn ɑːn/ · RUHN-ahn
Русский
слитный, без нужной пунктуации (о предложении)
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — verb + particle
Contrast
A grammar term: a run-on sentence pushes two sentences together with no period and no conjunction.

Of a sentence: made of two sentences joined without the right punctuation.

  1. Her essay was full of run-on sentences.
  2. Fix the run-on sentence in the second paragraph.
  3. A comma splice is one kind of run-on error.

runaway#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʌnəweɪ/ · RUH-nuh-way
Русский
беглый; неуправляемый; безоговорочный
Type
compound
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
A runaway is also a noun — a child or teenager who has left home.

Runaway has 3 distinct senses.

1. Having escaped or left home without permission. (беглый, сбежавший)

  1. The shelter takes in runaway teens.
  2. A runaway horse blocked the road for an hour.

2. Out of control and impossible to stop. (неуправляемый)

  1. A runaway truck hit the barrier on the hill.
  2. Runaway inflation wiped out their savings.

3. Won very easily; extremely successful. (безоговорочный, оглушительный)

  1. The movie was a runaway hit.
  2. She won the primary in a runaway victory.

running#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʌnɪŋ/ · RUH-ning
Русский
проточный; непрерывный, текущий
Type
participial
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ing from run
Contrast
In running shoes and running track, running is a noun used as a modifier, not an adjective. Three days running is an adverb meaning 'in a row'.

Running has 2 distinct senses.

1. Of water: flowing, especially from a tap. (проточный)

  1. The cabin has no running water.
  2. Rinse the rice under running water.

2. Continuing over a long time, with new parts added. (непрерывный, текущий)

  1. It's a running joke in our family.
  2. Keep a running total as you shop.
  3. They have a running argument about the thermostat.

runny#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʌni/ · RUH-nee
Русский
жидкий; сопливый, слезящийся
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — runnier, runniest

Runny has 2 distinct senses.

1. More liquid than it should be. (жидкий, текучий)

  1. The sauce came out runny.
  2. I like my eggs a little runny.
  3. This batter is runnier than the recipe says.

2. Of a nose or eyes: producing liquid because of a cold or an allergy. (сопливый, слезящийся)

  1. She has a runny nose and a cough.
  2. My eyes get runny every spring.

rural#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʊrəl/ · RUUR-uhl
Русский
сельский
Type
classifying
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
not gradable
Opposite
urban
Contrast
Rural is a neutral fact of geography; rustic adds old-fashioned charm. A city restaurant can look rustic, never rural.

In or about the countryside, away from cities.

  1. She grew up in rural Kansas.
  2. Rural hospitals are closing all over the state.
  3. The area north of the lake is still very rural.

rushed#

Pronunciation
/rʌʃt/ · RUHSHT
Русский
поспешный; в спешке
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more rushed, most rushed
Form
-ed from rush

Rushed has 2 distinct senses.

1. Done too fast, without enough time to do it well. (поспешный, скомканный)

  1. The ending of the movie felt rushed.
  2. We ate a rushed breakfast and ran for the train.

2. Of a person: forced to hurry. (торопящийся, в спешке)

  1. I felt rushed the whole morning.
  2. Don't let them make you feel rushed.

Russian Orthodox#

Pronunciation
/ˌrʌʃən ˈɔːrθədɑːks/ · ruh-shuhn OR-thuh-dahks
Русский
русский православный
Type
origin
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Russian православный on its own is Orthodox or Eastern Orthodox; Russian Orthodox names the Russian church in particular. Both words take a capital letter.

Belonging to the Orthodox church of Russia.

  1. They were married in a Russian Orthodox church in Brooklyn.
  2. His grandmother is Russian Orthodox.
  3. Russian Orthodox Christmas falls on January 7.

Russian-speaking#

Pronunciation
/ˌrʌʃən ˈspiːkɪŋ/ · ruh-shuhn-SPEE-king
Русский
русскоязычный
Type
compound; also origin
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — noun + -ing participle
Contrast
Russian-speaking is about the language, not the country: a Russian-speaking family may be from Kazakhstan or Israel. After a verb say They speak Russian.

Using Russian as an everyday language.

  1. The clinic is looking for a Russian-speaking nurse.
  2. There is a large Russian-speaking community in Brighton Beach.
  3. She joined a Russian-speaking book club.

rustic#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʌstɪk/ · RUH-stik
Русский
деревенский, простой (в хорошем смысле)
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more rustic, most rustic
Contrast
Rustic is a compliment about style — plain and country-like. Rural is only about location.

Plain and old-fashioned in a country way that people find charming.

  1. The restaurant has a rustic look — bare wood and iron lamps.
  2. We rented a rustic cabin with a wood stove.
  3. The bread is dense and rustic.

rusty#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʌsti/ · RUH-stee
Русский
ржавый; подзабытый (о навыке)
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — rustier, rustiest
Form
-y from rust

Rusty has 2 distinct senses.

1. Covered with rust. (ржавый)

  1. A rusty nail was sticking out of the board.
  2. The gate gets rustier every year.

2. Of a skill: weaker than it was because you have not practiced. (подзабытый, утраченный без практики)

  1. My Spanish is pretty rusty.
  2. He's rusty after two years off the ice.

saccharine#

Pronunciation
/ˈsækərɪn/ · SAK-uh-rin
Русский
приторный, слащавый
Type
evaluative
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more saccharine, most saccharine
Contrast
The adjective ends in -e: saccharine. Saccharin, with no e, is the noun for the artificial sweetener.

So sweet and sentimental that it feels fake.

  1. The movie ends on a saccharine note.
  2. He sang it in a saccharine voice.
  3. The card's message was a little saccharine for my taste.

sacred#

Pronunciation
/ˈseɪkrɪd/ · SAY-krid
Русский
священный, святой
Type
classifying; also quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more sacred, most sacred
Takes
to + person or group (sacred to the Lakota)
Contrast
The -ed is /ɪd/ and makes a second syllable: SAY-krid. Sacred covers religion and deep respect; holy is stronger and almost always religious.

Sacred has 2 distinct senses.

1. Connected with religion and treated with religious respect. (священный, святой)

  1. The mountain is sacred to several tribes.
  2. The choir sang sacred music at the service.

2. Too important to change, break or joke about. (неприкосновенный, святой)

  1. Sunday dinner is sacred in this family.
  2. Nothing was sacred in his comedy.

sacrificial#

Pronunciation
/ˌsækrɪˈfɪʃəl/ · sak-rih-FISH-uhl

Type
classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ial from sacrifice
Contrast
A sacrificial lamb is a set phrase for the person blamed or given up so that others are protected.

Offered up or given up, especially so that something else is saved.

  1. The manager became the sacrificial lamb after a bad season.
  2. Ancient texts describe a sacrificial altar on the hill.
  3. He made a sacrificial move to protect his queen.

sacrilegious#

Pronunciation
/ˌsækrəˈlɪdʒəs/ · sak-ruh-LIJ-uhs
Русский

Type

Position

Comparison

Contrast
Watch the spelling: sacrilegious has -rileg-, not -relig-, even though it is about religion.

Treating something holy — or something people deeply respect — with disrespect.

  1. Wearing shoes inside the temple would be sacrilegious.
  2. Putting ketchup on it is practically sacrilegious in Chicago.
  3. The church called the painting sacrilegious.

sacrosanct#

Pronunciation
/ˈsækroʊsæŋkt/ · SAK-roh-sangkt
Русский
неприкосновенный, священный

Position

So important that nobody is allowed to change or question it.

  1. In this office, Friday afternoons are sacrosanct.
  2. The contract is not sacrosanct — we can renegotiate.
  3. He treats his grandmother's recipe as sacrosanct.

safe and sound#

Pronunciation
/ˌseɪf ən ˈsaʊnd/ · sayf-uhn-SOWND
Русский
цел и невредим
Type
quality
Position
predicative only — after be, arrive, get home and similar verbs
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
A fixed phrase, always in this order and only after a verb: They got home safe and sound, never a safe and sound child.

Not hurt and no longer in danger, after something risky.

  1. The hikers were found safe and sound the next morning.
  2. Call me when you get there safe and sound.
  3. All three kids came back safe and sound.

sage#

Pronunciation
/seɪdʒ/ · SAYJ
Русский
мудрый
Type
quality
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Sage is also a noun — the herb, and a word for a wise old person. As an adjective it sounds slightly literary; everyday English says wise.

Wise, in a calm and experienced way.

  1. She gave me some sage advice about buying a house.
  2. The column is full of sage observations.
  3. A sage nod was all the answer I got.

sagging#

Pronunciation
/ˈsæɡɪŋ/ · SAG-ing
Русский
провисающий, обвисший; падающий
Type
participial
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more sagging, most sagging
Form
-ing from sag

Sagging has 2 distinct senses.

1. Hanging down in the middle under its own weight. (провисающий, обвисший)

  1. We had to replace the sagging porch roof.
  2. The old mattress was sagging in the middle.

2. Falling or getting weaker, about sales, prices or spirits. (падающий, слабеющий)

  1. Sagging sales forced the store to close.
  2. They tried to lift the team's sagging morale.

Saharan#

Pronunciation
/səˈhɛrən/ · suh-HAIR-uhn
Русский
сахарский
Type
origin; also classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-an from Sahara
Contrast
Sub-Saharan Africa means the part of the continent south of the desert, and is far more common in the news than Saharan alone.

Of or from the Sahara desert.

  1. A cloud of Saharan dust reached Florida last summer.
  2. The plant survives in Saharan conditions.
  3. Saharan nights can be surprisingly cold.

saintly#

Pronunciation
/ˈseɪntli/ · SAYNT-lee
Русский
святой, безгрешный, ангельский
Type
quality; also evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more saintly, most saintly
Form
-ly adjective from saint
Contrast
Saintly ends in -ly but is an adjective, not an adverb: saintly patience, never He smiled saintly.

Extremely good, patient and forgiving.

  1. She had saintly patience with the twins.
  2. Nobody is saintly at six in the morning.
  3. He was saintly about the whole mess.

salacious#

Pronunciation
/səˈleɪʃəs/ · suh-LAY-shuhs
Русский
непристойный, скабрёзный
Type
evaluative
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more salacious, most salacious
Contrast
Salacious is about sexual detail served up for gossip. It is stronger and far more disapproving than racy.

Full of sexual detail, in a way meant to shock or excite.

  1. The tabloid ran the salacious details on page one.
  2. The book was banned as salacious in 1928.
  3. He kept the story alive with salacious rumors.

salaried#

Pronunciation
/ˈsælərid/ · SAL-uh-reed
Русский
получающий оклад, на окладе
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ed from salary
Contrast
A salaried worker is paid a fixed amount per year; an hourly worker is paid by the hour. In the US the difference decides who gets overtime.

Paid a fixed yearly salary rather than by the hour.

  1. Salaried employees don't punch a clock.
  2. She moved from an hourly job to a salaried position.
  3. The company has about two hundred salaried staff.

salient#

Pronunciation
/ˈseɪliənt/ · SAY-lee-uhnt
Русский
заметный, существенный, главный
Type
quality
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more salient, most salient
Contrast
Nothing to do with salt. It means 'standing out as important', and is most often met in the salient points.

Standing out as the most noticeable or important.

  1. Let me go over the salient points of the contract.
  2. The most salient fact is that nobody was home.
  3. Her age is not salient here — her experience is.

saline#

Pronunciation
/ˈseɪliːn/ · SAY-leen
Русский

Type
classifying
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison

Contrast
Saline is a technical word from medicine and chemistry. Food that tastes of salt is salty, never saline.

Containing salt; made of a salt solution.

  1. The nurse flushed the line with saline solution.
  2. Wash the wound with a saline rinse.
  3. The lake is highly saline.

salted#

Pronunciation

Type

Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable

Contrast
Salted means salt was added — salted butter. Salty describes the taste: The soup is too salty.

With salt added, for taste or to keep it from spoiling.

  1. Use salted butter for the topping.
  2. A bowl of salted peanuts came with the drinks.
  3. Salted caramel is on every dessert menu now.

salty#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɔːlti/ · SAWL-tee
Русский
солёный; (разг.) обиженный, злой
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — saltier, saltiest
Takes
about + noun or -ing — in the slang sense
Form
-y from salt
Contrast
The slang sense is everywhere in American speech: Why are you so salty? means 'Why are you so annoyed?'

Salty has 2 distinct senses.

1. Tasting of salt, or containing a lot of it. (солёный)

  1. The soup is too salty.
  2. Ocean air feels salty on your skin.
  3. This batch is saltier than the last one.

2. Annoyed or bitter about something, in American slang. (обиженный, раздражённый)

  1. He's still salty about not getting picked.
  2. Don't get salty with me — I warned you.

Still able to be saved, repaired or rescued.

same#

Pronunciation
/seɪm/ · SAYM
Русский
тот же самый, одинаковый
Type
quality
Position
both — always with the, this or that: the same street, They look the same
Comparison
not gradable
Takes
as + noun or clause (the same as mine)
Opposite
different
Contrast
Always the same, never a same. Compare with as, not like and never than: the same as mine. Russian такой же and одинаковый are both the same: Our phones are the same.

Same has 2 distinct senses.

1. Not another one; exactly this one and no other. (тот же самый)

  1. We've lived on the same street for twenty years.
  2. She asked me the same question twice.
  3. Your jacket is the same as mine.

2. Not changed from before. (прежний, неизменный)

  1. The town looks the same as it did in 1990.
  2. He's still the same old Dave.

sanctimonious#

Pronunciation
/ˌsæŋktɪˈmoʊniəs/ · sangk-tih-MOH-nee-uhs
Русский
ханжеский, лицемерно-праведный
Type
evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more sanctimonious, most sanctimonious
Contrast
Sanctimonious is always an insult — it means acting morally better than everyone else. The neutral words are devout and religious.

Acting as if you are morally better than other people.

  1. Spare me the sanctimonious lecture about recycling.
  2. He gets sanctimonious whenever the topic comes up.
  3. The editorial struck a sanctimonious tone.

sandy#

Pronunciation
/ˈsændi/ · SAN-dee
Русский
песчаный; песочного цвета
Type
quality; also color
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — sandier, sandiest
Form
-y from sand

Sandy has 2 distinct senses.

1. Covered with sand, or made mostly of sand. (песчаный)

  1. We found a long sandy beach south of town.
  2. The soil here is sandy and dries out fast.
  3. The path got sandier closer to the dunes.

2. Of hair: light yellowish brown. (песочного цвета, светло-русый)

  1. He has sandy hair and freckles.
  2. Her eyebrows are sandy, almost blond.

sane#

Pronunciation
/seɪn/ · SAYN
Русский
в здравом уме; разумный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — saner, sanest
Opposite
insane
Contrast
The noun is sanity. In everyday speech insane often just means 'crazy' or even 'amazing': That concert was insane.

Sane has 2 distinct senses.

1. Mentally healthy; not mentally ill. (в здравом уме, вменяемый)

  1. The court found him sane enough to stand trial.
  2. Long walks are what keeps me sane.

2. Sensible and reasonable. (разумный, здравый)

  1. Let's set a sane deadline for once.
  2. A saner approach would be to test it first.

sanguine#

Contrast
A formal word. Everyday English says optimistic or hopeful.

Cheerfully confident that things will turn out well.

  1. She's sanguine about the company's chances.

sanitary#

Pronunciation
/ˈsænɪtɛri/ · SAN-ih-tair-ee
Русский

Type
classifying; also quality
Position

Comparison

Opposite

Contrast
Russian санитарный matches only the technical sense. English also uses sanitary to mean 'clean enough to be safe': That counter isn't sanitary.

Sanitary has 2 distinct senses.

1. Having to do with keeping places clean and free of disease. (санитарный)

  1. The kitchen failed a sanitary inspection.
  2. Sanitary conditions in the camp were poor.

2. Clean enough to be safe. (гигиеничный)

  1. Eating off that counter isn't very sanitary.
  2. They keep the prep area sanitary.

sanitized#

Pronunciation
/ˈsænɪtaɪzd/ · SAN-ih-tyzd
Русский
продезинфицированный; приглаженный, смягчённый
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more sanitized, most sanitized
Form
-ed from sanitize
Contrast
American spelling is -ized; British writing has sanitised.

Sanitized has 2 distinct senses.

1. Cleaned thoroughly so that germs are killed. (продезинфицированный)

  1. All the equipment is sanitized between classes.
  2. The tray came out sanitized and still warm.

2. With the unpleasant or embarrassing parts taken out. (приглаженный, смягчённый)

  1. The textbook gives a sanitized version of the war.
  2. It was a sanitized retelling for children.

An American informal word; the British equivalent is soppy. It teases rather than attacks.

Too sentimental, in a silly and slightly embarrassing way.

  1. We watched a sappy Christmas movie.

sarcastic#

Pronunciation
/sɑːrˈkæstɪk/ · sahr-KAS-tik
Русский

Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more sarcastic, most sarcastic
Form
-ic from sarcasm
Contrast
Sarcastic is saying the opposite of what you mean in order to mock someone. Ironic is wider and usually aimed at no one.

Saying the opposite of what you mean, to mock or criticize.

  1. 'Great job,' he said, sarcastic as ever.
  2. Don't be sarcastic with your teacher.
  3. She made a sarcastic comment about my parking.

sardonic#

Type
quality
Position

Comparison

Contrast
Sardonic is darker and quieter than sarcastic — a sardonic smile mocks without a word being said.

Mocking in a dark, unamused way.

  1. He gave a sardonic smile and went back to his coffee.
  2. The narrator has a sardonic sense of humor.
  3. Her tone was dry and sardonic.

sartorial#

A formal, often playful word. It describes choices, style and splendor — never a person directly: his sartorial taste, not a sartorial man.

Having to do with clothes and the way someone dresses.

  1. His sartorial choices got more attention than his speech.
  2. The piece is about sartorial trends in menswear.
  3. She arrived in full sartorial splendor.

sassy#

Pronunciation
/ˈsæsi/ · SAS-ee
Русский
дерзкий, бойкий
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — sassier, sassiest
Contrast
An American word. About a child it is a complaint; about an adult or a style it is usually a compliment.

Sassy has 2 distinct senses.

1. Cheeky and rude, especially about answering back. (дерзкий, нахальный)

  1. Don't get sassy with your grandmother.
  2. He was a sassy five-year-old.

2. Lively, bold and confident, often about style. (бойкий, задорный)

  1. She showed up in a sassy red dress.
  2. The ad copy is sassier than anything they've run before.

Contrast
Satiric is a rarer form of the same word. Do not confuse it with satisfactory or with sarcastic, which is about a person's tone, not a work.

Using humor and exaggeration to criticize people or institutions.

  1. The magazine runs a satirical column every week.
  2. It's a satirical novel about Wall Street.
  3. The piece is satirical, not a real news report.

satisfactory#

Type

Position

Comparison

Opposite

Contrast
Satisfactory means 'good enough', not 'good' — a satisfactory grade is barely passing. Satisfying is about pleasure: a satisfying meal.

Good enough for the purpose, but no better than that.

  1. The repair work was satisfactory.
  2. We need a satisfactory explanation by Friday.
  3. Her condition is listed as satisfactory.

saturated#

Pronunciation
/ˈsætʃəreɪtɪd/ · SACH-uh-ray-tid
Русский

Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more saturated, most saturated
Takes
with or in + noun
Form
-ed from saturate

Saturated has 2 distinct senses.

1. Completely soaked through. (пропитанный, промокший насквозь)

  1. My jacket was saturated by the time I got home.
  2. The ground is saturated after three days of rain.

2. Holding as much as it possibly can. (насыщенный)

  1. The market is saturated with delivery apps.
  2. Butter is high in saturated fat.
  3. The photo has deep, saturated colors.

savage#

Pronunciation
/ˈsævɪdʒ/ · SAV-ij
Русский
жестокий, свирепый; (сленг) беспощадный
Type
quality; also evaluative
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more savage, most savage
Contrast
Calling people savage in the old sense of 'primitive' is offensive today. The living uses are 'very violent' and, in slang, 'brutally blunt'.

Savage has 3 distinct senses.

1. Very violent or fierce. (жестокий, свирепый)

  1. The dog gave him a savage bite.
  2. It was a savage storm, even for February.

2. Extremely harsh, about criticism. (уничтожающий, резкий)

  1. The review was savage.
  2. She wrote a savage takedown of the proposal.

3. Brutally blunt in a way people find funny, in slang. (беспощадный, дерзкий)

  1. His reply was savage.
  2. That comeback was straight-up savage.

savory#

Pronunciation
/ˈseɪvəri/ · SAY-vuh-ree
Русский
несладкий, солоноватый; пикантный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more savory, most savory
Opposite
sweet
Contrast
American spelling savory; British savoury. Not savory about a person or a business means shady: a less than savory character.

Savory has 2 distinct senses.

1. Salty or spicy rather than sweet. (несладкий, солёный)

  1. Do you want something sweet or savory for breakfast?
  2. The bakery makes savory hand pies.

2. Tasting good in a rich, salty way. (пикантный, насыщенный)

  1. The broth was deep and savory.
  2. Mushrooms add a savory note to the sauce.

scant#

Pronunciation
/skænt/ · SKANT
Русский
скудный, едва достаточный
Type
quantity
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Scant goes straight before a noun, often before a number: a scant two weeks. After a verb use scarce or not enough.

Scant has 2 distinct senses.

1. Barely any; much less than is needed. (скудный, ничтожный)

  1. There is scant evidence for either theory.
  2. The report got scant attention from the press.

2. Just a little under the amount named. (неполный, с небольшим недобором)

  1. Add a scant teaspoon of salt.
  2. We finished in a scant three hours.

scanty#

Pronunciation
/ˈskænti/ · SKAN-tee
Русский
скудный; очень открытый (об одежде)
Type
quantity
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
-er/-est — scantier, scantiest
Contrast
About information scanty is close to scant, but scant only stands before a noun. About clothing only scanty works.

Scanty has 2 distinct senses.

1. Too small in amount. (скудный, недостаточный)

  1. The evidence is scanty at best.
  2. They lived on scanty rations that winter.

2. Of clothing: covering very little of the body. (открытый, куцый (об одежде))

  1. She wore a scanty costume for the number.
  2. The outfits got scantier as the show went on.

scarce#

Pronunciation
/skɛrs/ · SKAIRS
Русский
дефицитный, редкий
Type
quantity
Position
both — mostly after a verb
Comparison
-er/-est — scarcer, scarcest
Opposite
plentiful
Contrast
Scarce is about supply — there is not enough of it. Rare is about how seldom something occurs: a rare bird, but scarce housing. The adverb scarcely means 'hardly'.

Not available in the amount people need; hard to get.

  1. Affordable apartments are scarce downtown.
  2. Water became scarcer every week of the drought.
  3. Good news has been scarce this year.

Type

Position

Comparison

Contrast
Scarlet is a bright red leaning to orange; crimson leans to purple. Scarlet fever is a children's illness.

Pronunciation

Scattered has 3 distinct senses.

1. Spread out over an area instead of being together. (разбросанный, рассеянный)

2. Happening here and there rather than everywhere, about weather and outages. (местами, отдельный)

3. Of a person: unable to keep track of things. (рассеянный, несобранный)

  1. I'm a little scattered today — I didn't sleep.
  2. He sounded scattered on the phone.

schizophrenic#

Pronunciation
/ˌskɪtsəˈfrɛnɪk/ · skit-suh-FREN-ik

Type
classifying; also evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more schizophrenic, most schizophrenic
Form
-ic from schizophrenia
Contrast
The loose sense 'contradictory' offends many people, since it borrows a real illness as a metaphor. In writing, prefer contradictory or inconsistent.

Schizophrenic has 2 distinct senses.

1. Having or relating to schizophrenia, a serious mental illness. (шизофренический)

  1. He was treated at the clinic for schizophrenic episodes.
  2. The book explains what schizophrenic symptoms actually look like.
  3. Her brother is schizophrenic and has been stable for years.

2. Pulling in two opposite directions at once. (противоречивый, двойственный)

  1. The company's schizophrenic marketing sells luxury and thrift in the same ad.
  2. Policy on the region has been schizophrenic for a decade.

  2. Even seasoned climbers turn back in that weather.

2. Given flavor with salt, pepper or herbs. (приправленный)

  1. The soup was perfectly seasoned.
  2. Use lightly seasoned ground beef for the filling.

Contrast
Also a noun (a second of time) and a verb (to second a motion). In I finished second it is the adverb.

Second has 2 distinct senses.

1. Coming right after the first. (второй)

  1. Her office is the second door on the left.
  2. He struck out in the second inning.
  3. This is the second time this week.

2. One more of the same kind. (ещё один, второй)

  1. Give him a second chance.
  2. Would you like a second helping?

Contrast
Also a noun: keep a secret. The person who holds things back is secretive, not secret.

Secret has 2 distinct senses.

  1. There is a secret door behind the bookcase.
  2. She kept the letters in a secret compartment.

Secure has 3 distinct senses.

  2. The building is more secure than it looks.

  2. Check that the straps are secure.

3. Feeling confident and not worried. (уверенный, спокойный)

  2. He is secure in the knowledge that his family backs him.

self-contradictory#

Pronunciation
/ˌsɛlfkɑːntrəˈdɪktəri/ · self-kahn-truh-DIK-tuh-ree
Русский
противоречащий сам себе
Type
compound
Position
both — mostly after a verb
Comparison
more/most — more self-contradictory, most self-contradictory
Form
compound — self + contradictory

Containing two statements that cannot both be true.

  1. His testimony was self-contradictory from the first page.
  2. The rule is self-contradictory: it bans what the next line requires.
  3. That is a self-contradictory way to describe a free market.

self-defeating#

Pronunciation
/ˌsɛlfdɪˈfiːtɪŋ/ · self-dih-FEE-ting
Русский
контрпродуктивный, обречённый на провал
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more self-defeating, most self-defeating
Form
compound — self + the participle defeating

Working against the very thing you are trying to achieve.

  1. Cutting the training budget to save money is self-defeating.
  2. He is stuck in a self-defeating cycle of quitting and starting over.
  3. Their strategy turned out to be more self-defeating than risky.

self-deprecating#

Pronunciation
/ˌsɛlfˈdɛprəkeɪtɪŋ/ · self-DEP-ruh-kay-ting
Русский
самоироничный, самокритичный
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more self-deprecating, most self-deprecating
Form
compound — self + deprecating, 'putting down'
Contrast
Usually charming rather than sad: it is making fun of yourself before anyone else can.

Making light of your own abilities or achievements.

  1. His speech was funny and self-deprecating.
  2. She made a self-deprecating joke about her cooking.
  3. He is more self-deprecating in interviews than he is in person.

Harming yourself by the way you behave.

  1. Drinking before every shift was self-destructive and he knew it.
  2. The band fell apart in a self-destructive spiral.
  3. His choices got more self-destructive after the injury.
